**07/03/2026 13:25**  
**0003-07/03/2026-AmericaIsrael-Iran War — history, recent escalation, and global impact**

This is **not a sudden conflict**. What looks sudden in 2026 is really the **breakout phase of a long buildup** between three layers of confrontation:

1. **U.S.–Iran hostility** going back to the 1979 Iranian Revolution, sanctions, nuclear disputes, tanker incidents, militia attacks, and the 2020 killing of Qasem Soleimani. CFR’s long timeline shows this as a decades-long rivalry, not a new war. citeturn1search5turn1search2

2. **Israel–Iran shadow war** that ran for years through proxies, covert action, cyber operations, assassinations, and strikes in Syria, Lebanon, Iraq, and at sea. That changed in **2024**, when the conflict moved from mostly indirect confrontation to **direct state-on-state attacks**. After the April 1, 2024 strike on Iran’s consular building in Damascus, Iran launched more than 300 drones and missiles at Israel, its first direct attack on Israel from Iranian territory. citeturn0search5turn1search1

3. **Regional spillover system** involving Hezbollah, Iraqi militias, the Houthis, Red Sea shipping, and Gulf energy routes. So the 2026 war is best seen as the moment when the old proxy structure could no longer contain the confrontation. citeturn0search5turn0search2

### When did this “current war” effectively start?

For the **current shooting war phase**, Reuters reports that the major new escalation began on **February 28, 2026**, when the **United States and Israel launched strikes on Iran**. Reuters described that decision as a major escalation after Israel’s existing campaign and said the first phase targeted Iran’s command-and-control systems, naval forces, ballistic missile sites, and intelligence infrastructure. citeturn0search9turn1search0

So the clean way to think about it is:

- **Long history:** 1979 onward  
- **Direct Israel–Iran phase:** 2024 onward  
- **Full U.S.–Israel–Iran war phase:** **28 Feb 2026 onward** citeturn1search5turn0search5turn0search9turn1search0

### Why did it escalate so sharply now?

Several forces seem to have converged at once.

**First, the proxy war stopped being enough.** The 2024 direct exchanges broke an old barrier. Once Iran and Israel had already attacked each other directly, the psychological and strategic threshold was lower. citeturn0search5turn1search1

**Second, the nuclear issue and military targeting appear to have merged.** Reuters’ conflict mapping and reporting describe U.S. and Israeli strikes on Iranian nuclear-linked and military targets, which pushed the confrontation from deterrence signaling into an attempt to degrade Iran’s strategic capabilities. citeturn1search10turn0search9turn1search0

**Third, maritime chokepoints became part of the war.** Iran’s retaliation and the wider conflict disrupted the **Strait of Hormuz**, and Reuters says shipping there slowed to a near-halt. Once Hormuz is threatened, this stops being only a regional military issue and becomes an immediate global energy and trade issue. citeturn0search2turn0news36turn0news37

**Fourth, political signaling hardened instead of softening.** Reuters reports mention President Trump’s demand for Iran’s “unconditional surrender,” which markets interpreted as a sign that de-escalation might not come quickly. citeturn0news36turn1news37

### What is happening right now?

As of today, Reuters reports that:

- the war has widened enough to choke major **air and sea corridors** in the Middle East,  
- shipping through Hormuz has slowed dramatically,  
- insurers and governments are treating Gulf transit as a war-risk problem, and  
- the U.S. is offering up to about **$20 billion** in reinsurance support for maritime losses in the Gulf to keep some shipping confidence alive. citeturn0search2turn0search4

Reuters also reports major civilian harm inside Iran and continuing diplomatic efforts, but no settled resolution yet. citeturn1news37

---

## Global impact today

### 1) Oil

This is the biggest immediate shock.

The **Strait of Hormuz** is one of the world’s most important energy chokepoints. The IEA says that in **2025 nearly 15 million barrels/day of crude**, about **34% of global crude oil trade**, passed through Hormuz. It also says most of that flow was headed to **Asia**, especially **China and India**. citeturn1search6turn1search7

Reuters says the current war has effectively shut or heavily disrupted that route, with around **one-fifth of global crude and natural gas supply suspended** and oil up **24% in a week**, moving above **$90**. Barclays said Brent could test **$120**, and in an extreme scenario even **$150**. citeturn1news36turn0news36

That means:
- higher petrol and diesel prices,
- costlier aviation fuel,
- inflation pressure,
- stress for countries that import energy,
- windfall gains for some producers outside the disrupted zone. citeturn1news36turn0search6

### 2) Gas / LNG

Hormuz matters not only for oil but also LNG. The IEA says extended disruption would drive up global gas prices because countries dependent on UAE/Qatar cargoes would be pushed toward the spot market. citeturn1search7

So even countries far from the war can feel it through:
- electricity generation costs,
- industrial fuel costs,
- fertilizer and chemicals,
- winter storage pricing expectations. citeturn1search7turn0search6

### 3) Stock markets

Reuters says equities in the U.S. and Europe have fallen as the Iran war and rising oil prices hit sentiment. Markets are reacting to a classic wartime mix: **higher energy costs + supply uncertainty + weaker growth expectations + geopolitical fear**. citeturn0search1turn0search6

Typically in this kind of move:
- **airlines, transport, chemicals, autos, and energy-intensive industry** get pressured,
- **oil & gas producers, some defense names, and safe-haven assets** hold up better,
- broad indexes weaken if investors think inflation will re-accelerate. This last point is an inference from Reuters’ market coverage plus the energy shock. citeturn0search1turn1news36

### 4) Gold and safe havens

Reuters says gold rose as investors sought safety after the U.S.–Israel strikes on Iran. citeturn0search7

This is normal war-market behavior:
- money shifts toward **gold**,
- often toward **U.S. Treasuries** and the **dollar** too,
- riskier assets sell off first.  
The gold move itself is directly reported; the broader safe-haven pattern is a market inference consistent with that reporting. citeturn0search7turn0search1

### 5) Shipping, insurance, and trade

Reuters says the conflict has choked transport corridors and that maritime losses are serious enough for U.S. reinsurance support. That tells you the market is not only worried about price, but about **physical movement** and **insurability**. citeturn0search2turn0search4

That affects:
- tanker rates,
- marine insurance premiums,
- delivery times,
- refinery feedstock planning,
- container and air-cargo rerouting. citeturn0search2turn0news37

### 6) Europe

Reuters says Europe is bracing for an economic hit because higher energy prices can raise inflation and weaken already modest growth. Europe is less directly dependent on Hormuz than Asia, but not immune, because global prices are globally set. citeturn0search6turn1search6

### 7) Asia, especially India and China

This is where the dependence is structurally heavier. The IEA says China and India together received **44%** of the crude moving through Hormuz in 2025. That means Asia is the main transmission channel for the energy shock. citeturn1search6

For India specifically, the likely pressures are:
- higher oil import bill,
- rupee sensitivity if crude stays high,
- inflation risk,
- pressure on airlines, paints, chemicals, logistics, and OMC margins depending on policy response.  
The India exposure is supported by IEA flow data; the sector implications are economic inference from high crude sensitivity. citeturn1search6turn1news36

### 8) Countries that may benefit

Some countries and sectors can benefit, at least temporarily:
- oil exporters outside the war zone,
- alternative crude suppliers such as the U.S., Canada, Brazil, and Russia,
- defense contractors,
- shipping firms able to command wartime premiums. Reuters notes Asian refiners are already scrambling for alternative barrels from places like the U.S., Canada, and Brazil. citeturn0news37turn1news36

---

## Big picture: what changed in the world system?

The real change is not only that bombs are falling. It is that **three systems fused into one crisis**:

- **military confrontation**
- **energy chokepoint risk**
- **global inflation / market repricing**

That fusion is why this conflict feels bigger than a normal Middle East flare-up. Once Hormuz, Gulf shipping, and large-scale U.S. involvement are all in play at the same time, the impact travels from battlefields into:
- fuel prices,
- central-bank expectations,
- freight and insurance,
- airline routes,
- growth forecasts,
- household inflation. citeturn0search2turn1news36turn0search6

## Simple summary

**When did it start?**  
Deep roots from **1979**; direct Israel–Iran war from **2024**; current **U.S.–Israel–Iran war phase began 28 Feb 2026**. citeturn1search5turn0search5turn0search9turn1search0

**Why the sudden escalation?**  
Because years of proxy conflict, the 2024 direct attacks, nuclear/military targeting, and Hormuz disruption finally crossed into open war. citeturn0search5turn1search1turn0search2

**What is the biggest impact today?**  
**Energy and transport shock.** Oil, gas, shipping, insurance, and inflation are the main global transmission channels. citeturn1news36turn0search2turn0search4turn1search7

**Who gets hit most?**  
Energy importers, airlines, transport, industry, Europe’s growth outlook, and especially **Asian buyers** dependent on Hormuz-linked flows. citeturn1search6turn0search6turn0search1
